ParlaMint-CZ_2023-02-28-ps2021-055-01-001-901.u1	Confirmation of the duration of the legislative emergency
ParlaMint-CZ_2023-02-28-ps2021-055-01-001-901.u2	Madam President, my dear fellow Members, thank you for the floor. I would like to start by saying one thing about the procedure, so that we avoid unnecessary discussions or misunderstood during today's day. As has already been said by the President, the meeting was convened in accordance with Rule 99 of the Rules of Procedure, which means that the whole of Section 53 of the Rules of Procedure does not apply. To explain. This means that we will not vote on a possible meeting and vote after 19 or 21 or 24 hours at all, we are in the regime of Section 90 and this meeting is ongoing. So it's just to explain, so we don't argue here later whether the meeting should be suspended at 19 or 21 o'clock. That's what it's all about. And if I have entered into a legislative emergency debate, allow me only a brief statement and perhaps an explanation of what is mistakenly being heard in public space and in the media when many or even the public are asking whether it is justified to discuss the bill on the regulation of extraordinary valorisation in a legislative emergency. So let's just review what's been going on in the past few weeks. The government, and it's traceable from public sources, never questioned the fact that in 2023 it would have to proceed to extraordinary valorisation. That was never called into question. However, the government worked with the assumptions, with the prediction issued by the central bank - the Czech National Bank, which was also discussed by representatives of the National Economic Council of the Government, that the extraordinary valorisation would cost around CZK 15 billion, in the blackest assumptions around CZK 20 billion. Even from the mouth of the Minister of Finance, it was made clear that the state register was ready to deal with this item. It was also announced in advance that, of course, the state budget was not something that could not be adjusted in the context of inflation developments, and so on, during the financial year. February 10th - I repeat, it is important because very often it causes misunderstood - February 10th on Friday, I remember it very well because the Vice Prime Minister got the numbers of the Czech Statistical Office on the table here, sitting behind me, and I followed him when he saw the numbers and his skin color began to change dramatically and I began to worry about his health, so I understood that the numbers were far from corresponding to what was predicted by all the institutions, that is, when we convert it to those numbers, there was an increase in inflation, and so, as the Czech Statistical Office said on February 10th, by 8.6 percentage points. So dramatically more than was supposed to be. [[10.10 hours]] Well, because of this number, this figure also depends on the calculation of extraordinary valorization by law, so of course the load suddenly wasn't 15 billion, but it was 34.8 or 38.4 billion, it's not important now. Dramatically more. And from this point on, from this clear, Czech statistical office communicated data, all the following steps are taken by the government coalition, government as a whole, Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs. And since it is made clear once again that the government must decide, within 50 days of the government's regulation, on the extraordinary valuation and its size, within 50 days of the end of the month when this leap disturbance in inflation occurred, or since 31 January, it was also clear that the law must be effective by 22 March at the latest, that is, published in the Collection of Laws. That is why the government agreed at the moment when the representatives of the opposition with whom this was discussed - once again it is not true that they were not questioned, they were questioned - they clearly stated that they would not agree to discuss this draft regime § 90, when theoretically it would be manageable, and they had already announced in advance all possible obstructions, so the government really had nothing left but to use this extreme instrument, no one was happy about it, but this extreme instrument, that is, Section 99 and the discussion of the proposal in a state of legislative emergency. It is based on the judgment of the Constitutional Court of 1 March 2011, when, in a similar case, it was clearly stated that the conditions for examining a proposal in a state of legislative emergency were met, as defined by the Constitutional Court, because the rules of procedure clearly state in Section 99 that laws in a state of legislative emergency could be discussed if - I quote - the State is facing significant economic damage. And it's not just 19.4 billion tied to valorization on July 1st of this year. Of course, it has a convention for all other valorizations, and we come to the sum of CZK 100 billion. And I firmly hope that no matter what political club it belongs to, no one will doubt that there is serious economic damage. That is why the government has taken action in another regime not because of the opposition, so that we can fulfil the condition on 22 March, to discuss the state of legislative emergency. That is why I have taken the liberty of explaining clearly, perhaps clearly, all the reasons that led to this, Minister Jurečka and the government, and that is why I would ask you to support the state of legislative emergency, so that this does not go beyond debate, so that we do not actually get into an agreement. If we are in a state of legislative emergency, we are to act in a state of legislative emergency, or within a reasonable time frame, and I am not saying that we must vote in an hour, but in some reasonable time frame, because then we will truly, as legislators, honor what is written in the draft of this law. Thank you very much.

The ongoing pension financing system has been in crisis in recent years. Critics complain that the ongoing pension funding system is too rigid, that as a benefit to the welfare state it contributes to high unemployment, that pension insurance weakens incentives to work and reduces the growth potential of the economy. The ongoing system is an institution that makes the division of the pie more equal, but limits the size of the pie. The economists did not always take such a negative view. In the 1950's, most people who had experienced two world wars and a major economic crisis believed that an ongoing pension financing system was useful and correct. In a situation where wage and population growth exceeded the yield on the capital market, the decision to introduce an ongoing pension financing system after World War II seemed not bad, even if the current population trend could be predicted. The solution to the pension system crisis in adverse demographic trends is that governments are looking for longer savings, especially in tightening pension rights, sometimes in reducing the level of benefits. However, the implementation of these measures is politically and socially sensitive. Nevertheless, cosmetic modifications to the existing system in a number of countries seem not enough. While growth in pension spending slows them down, it may not mean a long-term solution. The debate on pension reform is a classic example of how economic policy theory works. [[Politically?]] Political parties are ahead of the proposals for a change to the existing system, which is considered unsatisfactory by some. However, when it comes to breaking bread and the details should be negotiated, it is usually found that the differences between the political parties are too great to make a meaningful consensus. Browning has shown that pension payments are not socially effective in the government's managed pension model, but are the result of an electoral process where the older generation outvotes the young. If there was a referendum on the introduction of compulsory pension insurance, the first generation would probably choose to establish an ongoing system, even without fulfilling Aaronův's so-called condition. Unfortunately, there are indications suggesting that the political preferences of the population regarding the functioning of the pension system will not change much in the near future. On the one hand, the population development puts an ongoing system on the brink of the abyss, so that alternatives are sought, ways of ensuring old age. On the other hand, the higher proportion of older people in the population shifts the age of a middle-ranking voter towards the interest of voters in the post-productive age, preventing radical reform of the pension system. However, the result of these pressures within the political system is rather minor cosmetic changes to the existing system. The form of pension reform naturally reflects different political views, including the state's role in interfering with citizens' lives. Nevertheless, it is not possible to neglect the purely economic rules governing the functioning of pension systems. The pension system is not moving in the vacuum, but it is based on a wide range of broader social factors, such as national traditions, historical development, experience and attitudes of living generations, economic level and international context, including ratified international conventions and membership of the Czech Republic in the EU. Its reform should therefore also take into account the social context and have political support. As regards the type of pension schemes, pension schemes may be broken down according to different criteria: compulsory or voluntary membership, public or private management, insurance or redistribution, a fund or an ongoing system, defined benefits or defined contributions. Assistance in old age can be provided through employers' funds, private pension funds, personal savings or life insurance. People can also invest in their own children or real estate. It is necessary to distinguish systems continuously financed, defined and systems continuously financed and defined by contribution and systems funded by a fund and/or fund systems defined by a batch. The pension system can also be created by combining these approaches. Therefore, it is not enough to talk only about an ongoing pension and financing system for pensions or fund systems or only about public or private systems. So let's see what these different pension systems have, what their advantages and disadvantages are. The advantage of public ongoing pension systems is that they are more resistant to inflation, are not subject to fluctuations in the capital market and enable the pension system to balance according to parameters. Their disadvantage is often high redistribution, mandatory contributions that have the character of taxes, and greater sensitivity to demographic developments. The advantage of fund private pension systems is greater individualisation, where benefits correspond to paid funds, easier adaptation to demographic trends and low tax burden. Among the disadvantages of the private pension pillar are that private pension funds cannot offer insurance against depression, capital market risks or unexpected inflation. There are two basic models within Western European pension systems: Bismarckův and Beveridgeův. Now let's see what pension reforms are like in the world. Pension reforms in Western European countries. The prevailing feature of pension reforms in Western European countries is their moderation. Pension reforms in Latin American countries, let's take a closer look at them. The origin of pension schemes in Latin America dates back to the early twentieth century. A few decades after the launch of the Bismarckův model in Germany, continuous systems became very popular even in Latin America. The relationship between contributions and benefits was relatively weak and the conditions for granting pensions were quite generous. It was easy to obtain both early retirement and disability pensions. Chile was the first country in Latin America to privatise its pension system. The Pinochetův regime replaced the existing ongoing system of private pension funds, which was mandatory for employees newly entering the labour market. For others he paid opt-out. Every citizen's contributions are collected in an individual account. The system is mandatory, capital and privately managed. The privileged pension systems of the military and police remained unaffected by the reform and since 2008 a solidarity pension has been introduced as a new pillar. In recent years, many Latin American countries have introduced different variants similar to the Chilean model. In Bolivia, El Salvador and Mexico, the original public system was closed down and replaced by a private pension fund system. In Bolivia, private pension funds were nationalized, which had previously replaced public pensions. There are two parallel systems in Peru and Colombia. In Uruguay, a newly created component of private pension funds is based on a mandatory supplement to a reformed public system. In Argentina, however, the fund system was nationalized. So let's take a look and have a little discussion and think about the choice between the ongoing and the fund system. The choice between the ongoing and the fund system is not an easy matter. In this section, I will analyze with you the standard Aaronův rule for the introduction of either an ongoing or a fund system. We will discuss here, ladies and gentlemen, the functioning of the ongoing pension system and analyse the strengths and weaknesses of the ongoing and the fund system. We will also mention the discussed impact of both pension systems on savings. So the starting point for the debate on the choice of pension system is so-called Aaronův rule, which means m + g > r, where on the right hand side of the equation r is as the rate of return of private pension funds on the capital market and on the left hand side the rate of yield in the continuous system, which is given by population growth m and the rate of growth of average wages g. If the income rate of private pension funds is higher than population growth and average wage growth rates, then the fund system should be more favourable. On the contrary, if population growth, the so-called Samuelsonův biological yield rate, and the average wage from which pension insurance is paid, is growing faster than the income of private funds, the ongoing system of financing pensions is more favourable. The real yield of private pension funds in the world is different. The example of Chile, where annual revenues exceeded 12 %, is rather an exception. Estimates based on macroeconomic data and the known distribution of Swiss pension fund portfolios' assets show that the average annual yield rate was 1,5 % in the 1970s and 1980s. The delay in the real yield rate for real wage growth was thus 1.7 %. Also, the pension saving system in Singapore generated relatively low returns between 1960 and 1990, around 2% a year. Given the growth in real wages exceeding 4 percent, it appears that the real interest rate did not guarantee too high a rate of return on investment in pension funds. In Malaysia, annual revenues amounted to 1 %. The yield of a portfolio of US shares and bonds, which generated 5.5 per annum between 1926 and 1992, is therefore to be taken with a reserve. If funds are mandatory, the government must strongly regulate the pension funds sector in order to guarantee minimum pensions from these funds. High regulation and investment in safe instruments, most often in government bonds, do not lead to very high returns. One of the disadvantages of the fund system is the limited ability to transfer savings without penalising before reaching retirement age to another form of savings. Even if Aaronův's condition is met, it still does not give a complete and definitive answer to the question of the effectiveness of pension financing methods, or more precisely whether and under what circumstances the intergenerational pareto efficient allocation of consumption is achieved rather by that or that method. Aaron assumed exogenous wages and interest rates, so the result is rather for a small open economy. More serious is that Aaronův's rule does not take into account mixed systems, i.e. systems composed partly of an ongoing system and partly of funds, or a changing rate of pension contributions. Aaron's analysis focused on examining the state of affairs, but did not take into account the transaction costs of the entire transition and distribution conflicts between generations. Pension systems are no longer single-pillars, because pension benefits are derived from different sources. How does Breyer correctly argue (?) in the 1992 book, when moving from an ongoing system to a private-saving fund system, it is generally impossible to compensate the first generation of pensions for the loss that will result from leaving the system without making other generations worse. The choice between different pension funding schemes cannot be made only on the basis of an efficient criterion, but also the distribution between generations should be taken into account. The analytical finding that, in adverse demographic developments, there is no way of painless pension reform in which all groups would improve is extremely serious. For example, Kovalčík and In the 2000 text, he promised that the young and middle generation could improve in appropriate reform, while the elders would not be offended. That's misleading. The conflict between generations cannot be eliminated by any genius pension reform. [[16.30 hours]] Now let's take a look at the functioning of the continuous system. The balance of the continuous system is given by the equation W = p.D, where S is the contribution rate, W is the average wage, N is the number of workers, p is the average pension and D is the number of pensioners. By combining these parameters, the continuous system can be compensated. In adverse demographic developments, the average pension, pay, raise the pension rate, or reduce the number of pensioners, for example, by increasing the retirement age can be reduced. The ongoing system shall be based on solidarity or equivalence. In the case of a batch defined system, it is based on solidarity. Where the system is defined by contribution, it shall be based on equivalence. Pension entitlement is thus given by participation in pension insurance, the amount of earnings and the number of years worked, with the premium being paid from earnings as a profit ceiling. Sometimes the pension system is a combination of these approaches. Although continuous systems are designed to ensure social solidarity from richer to poorer, redistribution between generations from rich to poor is not so strong, if any, as there is a positive correlation between the amount of lifelong earnings and the length of life of an individual. Its effect can even be a degressive place progressive. Both ongoing and fund systems are sensitive to different types of risks. The demographic system is more sensitive to ongoing systems, because sooner or later they are adjusted to reduce pension deficits. The risk of the capital market is more subject to the fund system. Different pension funds achieve different returns. Some may even fail and the government will likely play the role of lender of the last instance. In the United States, where they know enforceable law and functioning capital markets, there may be a more natural attachment to fund finance. In Central Europe, where we remember war, monetary reforms or the changing of political regimes, more confidence in even an imperfect ongoing system may not be so understandable. None can avoid the problem of stowaways, moral gambling, unfavourable selection, transaction costs. These problems affect all types of contracts. They are particularly serious in the case of pension contracts covering 60 years or more. Now let's look at the impact of both systems on savings. Population ageing has macroeconomic consequences. The level of savings, capital stocks and growth is reduced and the labour market is negatively affected. These negative consequences will occur both in the ongoing system and in the fund system. It can be agreed with Holman of 2002, that the economic burden resulting from demographic ageing is completely independent of the form of a pension system. The impact of social insurance on savings is questionable. In Feldstein, Feldstein argued that social security was weakening private savings and had a negative impact on economic growth. On the other hand, the fund system, according to him, contributes to an increase in saving rates and accelerates economic growth. Felstein's fund savings or Feldstein? - it was supposed to be mandatory because some people aren't responsible enough, they won't be saving for retirement, and they'll fall into social media. Barro, on the other hand, expressed his hypothesis in 1978 that in the dynastic model of the family with intergenerational transfers, the effect of social insurance on savings would be neutral. Optimizing actors will balance government policy. Government debt will be redeemed with higher taxes in the future. The mandatory state pension system does not create any new social wealth, it merely crowds out voluntary private savings. The truth, according to the Barro, is that the privatisation of the state pension system, if financed by an increase in government debt, does not in itself affect national savings. The increase in private savings in compulsory pension funds is fully pushed out by the decline in public savings. Only if the reform is financed at least in part by government financial consolidation could national savings increase. If the reform of the pension system increases national savings, this is not due to a change in the method of financing, an ongoing system versus a fund system, but to budgetary consolidation. Although funds replace the previous mandatory ongoing system, people perceive compulsory pension funds as a substitute for voluntary savings. The impact of the fund system on savings may not be positive. The biggest concern of a person investing in a pension fund, whether voluntarily or on a compulsory basis, is that he can't deal with his savings until retirement. Mandatory savings have certain shortcomings. For example, it may force some people to become more indebted or to divert funds from others... necessary expenses, such as raising children or investing funds into their own property or business. Now let's look at the next chapter at the cost of the transition. [[16.40 hours]] In a number of developed countries, state pension expenditures range between 10 and 15 percent of GDP. Since transition costs are non-zero, Aaronův's rule of choice between the interim and the fund system cannot be clearly applied. The 1998 Bulíř opinion that the fund system will be paid within one generation and, moreover, the development of the financial capital market will bring positive externality stimulating savings is too optimistic. Although the benefit of the fund system should be the stimulation of savings, the reality may be less pink. Most advocates of the fund system stress that this system has significantly increased savings in Chile, for example. However, this is not clear at all, because the rise in private savings in pension funds is balanced by the decline in other voluntary private savings. The beneficiaries of the transition to the fund system do not appreciate the costs of the transition that arise in connection with radical pension reform. Among the generations of young people who would enter the new system, and the generations of older ones who would remain in the ongoing system, there is a so-called lost generation that would apply twice: on their own pensions and on the pensions of their parents. The burden of this lost generation is proposed by the authors to address the issue of so-called debt recognition, which would finance the entire reform. But the problem is who and when would pay off this debt. According to Ricardo-Barroův's theorem of equivalence, government debt will sooner or later be redeemed with higher taxes. The belief that a lost generation could increase its well - being during its life - style is therefore illusionary. We assume that the cost of the transition will be financed by issuing debt recognition in the amount of D, which will increase the existing debt. The funds the government borrows to finance the transition will be invested and turned into savings in private pension funds. At the time of the pension reform, therefore, the newly accumulated capital is exactly equal to the newly created debt D. D is equal to S. Just as I said, government debts would sooner or later be redeemed by higher taxes. At least we see what awaits us here with this government. After N years at the interest rate debt would grow to D, in parentheses 1+I to entou, while savings would be evaluated to S, which is 1+I to entou. If the interest rate on the bond was equal to the annual rate of appreciation of funds in private pension funds, it would still be true that D equals S. The idea that the issuance of bonds of recognition could increase overall well-being is therefore illusionary. The objection may be that the interest rate on government debt I may not be equal to the annual appreciation of funds in pension funds J. Optimists would say that J will be higher than I, this is the annual appreciation of funds in funds will be higher than the interest rate on debt. In fact, such a assumption may not apply. The huge amount of free capital accumulated in pension funds can reduce interest rates, see Kubíček 2004. The rise of government deficits to bearable levels would, in turn, undermine financial markets that would demand greater returns for investing in bonds. Although it is not a priori clear which situation will arise, it is quite possible that the average rate of inflation from debt and annual appreciation will cancel each other out. It is possible to agree with Vostatek from 1998, that the fund system kind of resembles perpetuum mobile. If we have capital, we can have a return on capital. However, in order to obtain it, we must first save it. So either we can maintain a relative pension in the future, and then we have to save more and less to consume today, or we are going to consume today just like last generations, and then we have to accept lower pensions. The claim by Kreidl of 1998 that a system with a higher weight of fund financing could bring higher pensions with the same contributions is therefore unbelievable. This finding is based solely on a static comparison of pensions, from accumulated pensions to income from the interim system, while completely ignoring that people will have to pay higher taxes for the recognition bonds once, which would reduce normal consumption or higher inflation, which would devalue savings. The calculations of the implicit debt and its comparison with the explicit debt are rather misleading. First of all, it needs to be realised that implicit debt is fictional, that is, it is calculated as a hypothetical debt that would arise if the parameters of the continuous system were not changed. However, parameters will not change over time. Second, however, radical pension reform with the help of debt recognition is worse, as it turns this implicit debt into explicit. The estimates of the cost of the transition to the Fund's system are huge. If current pensioners are to receive an old-age pension like today and the middle-generation are to receive at least part of the pensions from the interim system, the costs of the transition range from one half to twice the GDP, although the authors are comforting that the implicit debt in the interim system is even higher, i.e. fictitious, while their debt is real. It can be agreed with the 1998 Kreidl that the mechanical maintenance of the ongoing system by means of higher taxes completely ignores the effects on the whole economy: tax evasion, unemployment and labour market distortion. The result would be a slowdown or a halt to GDP and wages growth. However, it is all the more surprising to resolve the pension reform by means of recognition bonds, which would have practically the same effect, as they would have to be repaid once. Whether you like it or not, the current young and middle generation cannot waive its responsibility for pension reform. They either pay it in the form of higher taxes or higher taxes in the future. [[unintelligible]] The idea that he can lie out of his responsibility or even reach higher pensions is unrealistic. No reform of the pension system can fundamentally solve this fundamental problem of intergenerational conflict. It is therefore difficult to agree with Holzmann's view of 1996 that the aim of pension reform is to make Paretian improvements. paretý-efficient shift would occur in a situation where at least a group could improve without any other group being offended. This, however, is difficult to fulfill in the face of adverse demographic developments, in which all generations will inevitably suffer relatively worse. The truth is, Bezděk, of 2001, that austerity measures in pension systems lead to a deterioration in the standard of living of future pensioners compared to the current situation. If today's middle and young generation wants to maintain a standard of living at an older age, it will have to limit its consumption today. Therefore, if people optimize consumption in two periods, productive and postproductive, their overall well-being will not increase. Of course, this does not rule out that specific individuals who are wise enough to save enough money for a pension will be looking forward to resting on the same or even higher relative pensions than their parents. Overall, however, each generation, thanks to the current pension system crisis, will have to make sacrifices. It will consume less in productive periods or in retirement. No lunch is free. [[16.50 hours]] It can be agreed with Schneider's view of 1998 that the ageing population will have a negative impact on economic growth over the next 20 years and there will be a decline in investment and real wages due to taxes needed to finance pensions. However, its estimate of an increase in economic potential of 0,5 percent of GDP thanks to the Fund's system is rather speculative. Even with the introduction of a fund system, the economy would bear the cost of demographic ageing. Kotlikoff's claim of 1996 on the positive impact of privatisation of the pension system on economic growth is therefore questionable. If reform were accompanied by budgetary consolidation, it could be right for a long period of time. But the increase in debt resulting from the reform will have the opposite effect. Too short a time series to demonstrate clearly the link between pension reform and economic growth. Although Gramm also supports the privatisation of the pension system in 1998, his proposal for reform is more realistic than Kotlikoffův. It does not borrow for the transition, but introduces a special saving rate and leaves the US social security rate unchanged for 2060. Thus, it is not the young and middle generation that will save their standard of living, but it is moving towards reform more transparently and responsibly. And let's go to the next part. In this section, the authors of the publication here - I stress once again that it does not copy the opinion, the political opinion of the SPD, it is just one of the many papers that is certainly good only to know about it. In this section we will outline the basic model of pension reform in the Czech Republic, as shown by the author's previous analysis. We will outline the basic parameters of the new pension scheme, which is based on the calculation of the executive pension reform team. We will then look at the question of whether the ongoing system is to penalize the childless in order to offset at least a little adverse demographic developments. So basic philosophy. One can make sure of the age of three ways. First, it can postpone current consumption and create private savings, money deposits, stocks, bonds, as well as private pensions. Second, one can provide for old age by producing and raising responsible children. When children are young, parents provide them with food, clothing, and education. When children grow up, they return gifts to parents in the form of cash or material transfers. Third, a person participates in a social security program. It is wrong when the debate on old-age security is constantly focused only on the third part. It should not be dominant in a free society, and certainly not the only source of security in old age. State pensions will fall over the next decades in all countries that record adverse demographic developments. This is valid regardless of the type of pension reform that the country will undergo. The transformation process in the first half of the 1990s created a pension scheme based on two pillars: a compulsory pension scheme in which the principle of solidarity is applied and the financing of which is ongoing, and a voluntary supplementary pension scheme with a state contribution designed as an individual savings and based on capital financing. Between 2002 and 2006, a group was formed at the Ministry of Finance, which calculated the balance of the pension system according to the options submitted by the political parties. The view that the pension system needs to be reformed has prevailed among democratic political parties. However, the parties' proposals differed. While the ODS presented the concept of equal income, ČSSD recommended a Swedish model with fictional accounts. The Swedish model with fictitious accounts, which binds the pension paid more to the contributions paid, is rather a cosmetic change. Although this change is a step towards greater addressability, the question is why people should pay their contributions to the ongoing system rather than directly to private funds. On the contrary, the ongoing system should ensure solidarity, voluntary savings should take into account the income and performance of the taxpayer. The debate on pension reform is now facing two extremes: on the one hand, doing nothing, on the other hand, radical reform. They're both wrong. The UK pension system is quite complicated. The system is multi-pillary and very complex. In general, it includes a range of products, i.e. state, corporate or private, batch or contribution-defined, variously responsible parties. By those parties, I don't mean the political parties, but the state, the employer, and so on. Staff members are obliged to plan their own pension and bear all risks, including investment risk, interest risk and longevity risks. The pension system in Great Britain offers, above all, one equal dose with universal coverage, the so-called basic state pension. The basic pension is supplemented by a number of tested benefits. Employed persons have basic security extended by the pension component tied to age, second state pension and so on, other pillars include corporate and personal pension plans. In the first pillar are the basic state pension and the second state pension and the tested benefits. In the second pillar are defined corporate income, defined corporate income, personal pension or collectively negotiated individual plans and capital share pensions. The third pillar represents additional voluntary contributions to business plans and separate, separate, contribution-defined pension accounts. Workers and self-employed persons contribute to the first pillar, which is publicly and continuously funded. Insurance is 10 per cent for the employee and 11.9 per cent for the employer. In addition to the basic state pension, there is a graduated old-age pension based on earnings since 1978. In the 1990s, the ratio of the average state pension versus wage was 25 per cent, today falling to up to 20 per cent. Pensions in Britain are subject to taxation, the increase in income on dependent children is not taxed. They're indexed by price. The second pillar offers business schemes, agreed personal pension insurance and new equity insurance. There is no formal commitment to participate in the second pillar. The pension entitlement arises at 65 years of age. The pensions from the second pillar can be defined both by contribution and by dose. Staff members may participate in one of the schemes of the second pillar, to withdraw from the second state pension. Individual plans that can be tied up are defined by contribution. A staff member who is entitled to a second national pension shall pay the same contribution rate as a non-tied worker. But the disposition of these funds is different. [[17.50 hours]] From contractual outs, part of the pension contributions of workers shall be transferred to his individual pension account, but his future pension from the first pillar shall be reduced. Today, the share of citizens using contractual outs is at least three fifths. The British Government serves as a settlement centre for contributions made to the first, so contributions made to the second pillar. Each worker participating in the second pillar of a business scheme shall have an account with his employer and a company which receives contributions to the second pillar, usually a pension fund or insurance company. Those who take part in the second pillar and want to draw benefits must ask for their payment between 60 and 75 years of age. Pension benefits purchased from an insurance company and individual accounts are indexed up to 5 per cent per year. If an individual is married or is married and the partner dies, the company must pay the widower or widow half the benefits that the deceased husband or wife received. The insurance product market is developing dynamically. In 2004, two large British insurance companies sent hundreds of thousands of clients letters telling them that the benefits would be low and advised them to stop making pension contributions and return to the state social security system. Half a million people actually listened and returned to the first pillar where they could draw a second state pension. The advantage or disadvantage of transferring funds from the State pillar to the private or corporate pillar depends on the situation on the financial markets. When prices on the capital market rise, contracting out becomes attractive, while when stock prices fall, clients are terrified of depreciation of savings and humbly return to the state system. When the bad situation in the markets gets longer, there is increasing political pressure to allow clients to return to social insurance, although otherwise they would not be allowed to do so. The first pillar covers all active-age citizens and their employers who are obliged to send pension contributions to the social insurance undertaking. The selected contributions are used by the State to pay the pensions of current pensioners, so the system works on the principle of PAYG. The amount of the maximum pension is limited to the maximum basis for premium payments. In the first pillar, there is a gradual increase in the retirement age of 60 for both men and women. There will be a stronger link between pensions and contributions. Doses are indexed according to inflation and wages. There is a possibility of later retirement, which will be affected by a reduction of 0.5 per cent for each month before the standard retirement age, and vice versa, the later retirement is favoured by a bonus of 0.5 per cent per month. The second pillar is the compulsory capitalization pillar of pension savings. It shall be introduced from 1 January 2005. The system is defined by contribution. It is to be attended by all citizens of active age, so that half of the contributions that have been paid to the social insurance company so far will be paid into their pension account. In Slovakia, 30% of the funds invested in personal pension accounts are to be invested. The transition to the new system was not easy. People were divided into two groups. The first group are citizens who were 16 years and fewer when the reform was introduced. They entered the second pillar at the time of taking up their first job. They then paid 9 per cent of the gross wages to the social insurance undertaking and 9 per cent to the pension fund. People over 16 years of age could decide whether they wanted to enter the pension pillar or not. Within a certain time, the people who joined the second pillar were allowed to rejoin it and rejoin the first pillar. The third pillar of the Slovak pension system is voluntary. Each citizen may contribute from his income to supplementary pension savings at his own discretion. The products have been modernised, making room for retirement and other forms - through life insurance, savings in banks, mutual funds, etc. The contribution to the third pillar favours the State tax. The trust of the Slovák in the new system is illustrated by a large number of taxpayers who volunteered to enter the second pillar. In the first half of 2008, 1.5 million people saved to the second pillar. The Ministry of Labour has not recommended entry into the new system to people who have been retired for less than 10 years, because the minimum saving period is just 10 years. The recommended period is at least 18 to 20 years, when entry into the second pillar should become advantageous. The pension management company may invest the money in the account only through the client of the selected pension fund. Pension management companies have created three pension funds, growth, balanced and conservative to secure the capital component of the pension system. In the event of a company's bankruptcy, pension funds are separated from the company's assets, so the fund itself would be transferred to another administrator. Money in the pension account is partly subject to inheritance. At death during the active life of the survivors, they receive a survivor's pension from the first pillar and inherit the pension fund's balance. However, if someone dies at retirement age, the savings on the pension account will fall to the pension management company. The survivors may only apply for a survivor's fund from the first pillar. So the biggest problem here, according to the author, is the large differences between old and new pensioners' pensions. The greatest threats to the system are high administrative costs of managing individual accounts, which often exceed the low returns of invested funds. When administrative costs and inflation rates are deducted from nominal returns, often the client gets negative. Whether or not someone's pension savings are paid is mainly due to the estimated saving time. This is a period when a citizen is employed and regularly contributes to a personal pension account. Experience has shown that pension management companies may also report real impairments, i.e. below inflation, compared to the initial assumptions of the regular assessment of pension savings. Pension reform in Slovakia substantially strengthened the principle of equivalence and weakened the principle of solidarity. Not only pensions on individual accounts, but also pensions in the ongoing system depend on contributions to the system. Therefore, the scope of redistribution will be reduced. It goes from applying an unjustifiedly high degree of solidarity to highlighting merit and strengthening individual responsibility for the post-retirement situation. In calculating pensions, the former Slovak pension system only took into account the five best-paid years of the last ten before the pension. This put the above groups at a disadvantage and favoured the low-income groups. Now not only the second pillar pension paid by lifelong contributions, but also the first pillar pension is calculated on the basis of paid premiums for a longer period of time. After the start of Prime Minister Robert Fic Slovakia made significant changes in the pension system. So far, Slovakia has been facing a second pillar of 9 per cent gross wages, with upcoming changes ending 3 to 4 per cent in private accounts, the rest going back to the social insurance company. Slovakia thus partially returned to the proven first pillar and weakened the second pillar of the pension system. [[18.30 hours]] Although the main objective of Slovak, but also of Hungarian and Polish pension reform was to ensure the long-term financial sustainability of the pension system, the problems are the resulting deficits during the transitional period. The transfer of part of social security to private pension funds was not free of charge. On the other hand, the Czech Republic has chosen a more modest approach to pension reform, which minimises budgetary costs over a longer period of time. Summary. So here I have outlined models of pension reform just as an example in several countries. Also, countries support the development of the second and third pillars of pension security, because pensions from the first state pillar, given the demographic situation of future pensioners, will not be enough. Recently, the popular transition to fictional virtual accounts - NDC. Although this principle leads to a significant strengthening of the principle of equivalence, the basic problem of the deficit is unable to address. At the turn of the millennium, pension reform did not change the system, yet it represented a significant shift because it strengthened the importance of employee and private pension plans that have so far played a marginal role. In international comparison, Britain is out of line with low state pensions and greater roles in employee and personal pension plans. Although the principle of equivalence in European pension systems is being strengthened, the principle of solidarity is not completely abandoned. State pensions still work to redistribute money from the richer, even though the rate of redistribution has decreased slightly in recent years. Providing in old age becomes more of a private concern than it was in the past. The state pension rather plays the role of a social safety net. What I would like to say is that the Constitutional Court found out about pensions. Pension reform in the Czech Republic after 2010 significantly influenced the Constitutional Court's decision on the right to adequate material security of 23 March 2010. And that was the case, and I'm sure many of you remember it, that was the case where he complained about the former judge to whom he was awarded as 1. 2. 2006 full invalidity pension of CZK 13 346, on the fact that the total amount of the pension granted is only 19 per cent of its previous income, which is not considered adequate material security. And the provisions of Section 15 on the reduction limits of the Pension Insurance Act according to the complainant cannot stand and are unconstitutional, since this provision is damaged by higher-income insured persons. The complainant recalled that according to the name of the Pension Insurance Act, it is insurance, i.e. a legal institute which has its fixed content in each legal state. In his view, the insurance paid should be treated as insurance, not as tax. By applying the second reduction limit in particular, there is a substantial reduction in the calculation base, without the clear reasoning of the legislator on the legitimate objective and meaning of such legislation. The subject matter of the review of the Constitutional Court was whether the ratio between the amount of pension insurance premiums and the amount of pensions for existing legislation meets the constitutional requirement of the so-called proportionality of material security for old age and does not give rise to the alleged inequality of pension insurance participants. The Constitutional Court acknowledges that there is no prerequisite for a direct link between the amount of insurance paid and the amount of benefits received in the future. Even in the interim system, the equivalence rate of the granted benefit and the previous income may be higher and lower at the time of economic activity. The Constitutional Court does not in any way call into question the principle of intergenerational solidarity in relation to the pension system, since it is built on it as a system continuously funded. [[19.00 hours]] However, the subject of the assessment must be solidarity of the intra-generational income, reflecting the uniform percentage rate of premiums paid by the economically active population. Therefore, high-income population groups are taking significantly higher amounts of money than low-income groups into the pension benefit scheme each year, notes the Constitutional Court. In line with the differences in commercial and public insurance, for which it is not possible to rely on the principle of net merit, the amount of the benefits fully reflects the amount of the premiums, according to the Constitutional Court, the ratio between the amount of premiums paid and the amount of pension granted must reflect a certain degree of proportionality for individual participants in order to comply with the constitutionally conformal requirements of Art. This Regulation shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union. 1 Charter of Fundamental Rights and Freedoms. The system has long been able to guarantee a satisfactory compensation ratio for individuals with income moving around the average wage, and favours those with wage below average, which is understood as a justified advantage. It can do so only at the cost of reducing the individual compensation ratio for persons with a double or multiple average wage. The system also guarantees them from a general point of view a dose that represents a certain minimum for dignified survival in old age. However, it is a benefit which cannot guarantee the maintenance of the standard of living usual for these persons from the period of economic activity, argues the Constitutional Court. So, when I return to the Constitutional Court's finding of the pensions in relation to this particular case, the Constitutional Court stated that the legislation implemented by the contested provision of Section 15 of Law No. 155/1995 Coll. creates a situation where a participant in a pension scheme contributing a three-fold amount to a participant contributing an amount calculated from the average wage is granted a pension of relatively less than half the amount. Legal regulation in order to ensure adequate material security for all members of pension insurance does not provide parts of insured persons with adequate material security reflecting the principle of merit, i.e. fulfilling the stimulating function of social policy. It is, by the way, the founding of the Constitutional Court 8/07 p. 17. Thus, the Constitutional Court concluded that the construction referred to in Section 15 of the Pension Insurance Act, which provides for two reduction limits at the same time as the absence of a ceiling, creates a significant proportion between the amount of the contribution to the insurance system, the amount of income and the amount of pension benefits granted in respect of a part of the insured persons, thereby violating the provisions of Articles 1 and 3 paragraph of the Charter (?). ParlaMint-CZ_2023-02-28-ps2021-055-01-001-901.u260	Thank you for the floor. Ladies and gentlemen, colleagues, I have been told by people that they are very interested in the passage that President Okamura called here between 17.20 and 18.35 and that they would like to hear it again. But don't worry, I'll text them where to find her. I won't say it again. Since this is a legislative emergency, I will not talk directly about pensions and things that we will discuss when we open this meeting. But allow me to take a short excursion just until the adoption of laws in a legislative emergency, until the use of legislative emergency in the Czech Chamber of Deputies, and how legislative emergency should probably be used or not. Allow me to begin with one bonmot attempt. The Government argued in its proposal for an amendment to the Pension Insurance Act relating to the special-purpose and social changes to the remuneration scheme for pensions by arguing that the allegedly unexpected increase in inflation and the resulting increased entitlement to cover the statutory valuation would cause significant economic damage. [[21.30 hours]] I don't know. The Minister of Finance Zbyněk Stanjura then publicly stated in Sunday's Questions to Václav Moravec that even in the current state budget, money for pension valorization will be found, even if this government amendment is not adopted, i.e. that it will find money in the budget without amending itself, to valorize pensions according to the current and effective pension insurance law. It can therefore be said that the most fundamental argument yet to reject the use of the state of legislative emergency for the discussion of the government amendment to the pension law and for rejecting the amendment as such was put forward by the Minister of Finance of the current government, and I would like to thank him publicly for that. Because we will certainly use this statement in our possible constitutional complaint against this amendment if the government coalition accidentally pushed it through the legislative process. And now, to the substance of the matter, the issue of the state of legislative emergency. This issue cannot logically be separated from its broader constitutional contexts. Each state's constitutional right is based on the definition of the fundamental rights and freedoms of citizens, and the constitutional regulation of fundamental rights and freedoms is determined primarily by the factual possibilities of this state regulation and the resources available to the State. Moreover, the constitutional regulation of fundamental rights and freedoms must operate in accordance with other normative systems, such as morality, ethics, justice, social traditions, etc. The core of the vast majority of the constitution is the constitutional regulation of the relationship between public authority and individual. Our Constitution does not specify this relationship directly, especially because it has failed to reach agreement on any relevant amendments to the Charter of Fundamental Rights and Freedoms. Therefore, a compromise solution has been adopted, according to which this Charter is not directly part of the Constitution, but is part of the so-called constitutional order. However, this does not change the legal nature of the regulation of fundamental rights and freedoms. Thus, the Charter is not only a Charter of Fundamental Rights but also a Charter of Freedoms. It also often confuses the concepts of law and freedom. It is therefore necessary to understand in the Charter the provisions which give rise to certain rights of citizens towards the State. Freedoms are then linked to the definition of autonomous spaces in which the State should not, or the public power to intervene, such as property, privacy and the like. Another difference between rights and freedoms is that, in the case of fundamental rights, it is necessary to point to their anchoring in the constitutional regulation, whereas this is not necessary in the case of freedoms. Individual rights are then based on legislation and must be demonstrated in order to apply them to public authorities. On the contrary, in the case of freedom, the public authority should prove that the law prohibits individuals from acting or, on the contrary, commands them to do so. The Charter of Fundamental Rights and Freedoms is created on the natural legal concept of fundamental rights, as evidenced by its preamble, which explicitly emphasises the recognition of the inviolability of the natural rights of a person and of the rights of a citizen. It also mentions the generally shared values of humanity. And now we will move on to the constitutionally-legal description, the analysis of the assumptions and conditions of the so-called state of legislative emergency, which we are talking about here in connection with the amendment to the Act on Pensions, and the so-called shortened legislative process, which, by its decision, provided for the discussion of the amendment to the Act on Pension Insurance by the President of the House, Mrs Markéta Pekarová Adamová. The negotiation of a draft law or its amendment to a short-term act in a state of legislative emergency constitutes a statutory exception to a proper legislative procedure, the justification of which is based solely on the gravity of the situation which, in the Government's view, must be immediately addressed by the adoption of a law or by its amendment. Thus, not in the mere opinion of the government, or even in the vote by the current House of the majority. Such circumstances and reasons, however, did not occur in relation to the amendment to the Pension Insurance Act. And perhaps the Minister Stanjura confirmed this unintendedly in the questions already mentioned by Václav Moravec, as I demonstrated at the beginning of this speech. Paragraph 99 (2) (a) is replaced by the following: By defining the reasons for declaring a state of legislative emergency, the Rules of Procedure of the Chamber of Deputies did not limit the application of this procedure to certain specified circumstances, but determined the intensity of their seriousness. This shortened conduct is thus conditional on the existence of exceptional circumstances and the intensity of possible negative consequences in the case of a standard negotiation of a draft law to one of the generally defined protected values, namely the rights and freedoms of citizens, the security of the State or the protection of property. As an exceptional circumstance within the meaning of the Constitutional Court's finding, only a circumstance which is clearly out of the ordinary course of political processes internal and external, or a circumstance such as natural disasters or epidemics may be considered, I add. And you know that the SPD never questioned the use of the Institute of Legislative Emergency whenever it was necessary in case of an epidemic of covid-19 to promptly approve any measures to help citizens, businessmen or firms. There was a clear reason and decision every day. In the case of government efforts to reduce the extraordinary valorisation of pensions, there is no reasonable or legal reason. Does the government think that raising pensions is some kind of natural or other disaster? Does aid to citizens, many of whom, according to recent sociological surveys, live below or near the income poverty threshold, regard as damage to the state budget? If so, that's terrifying for me. The justification for declaring a state of legislative emergency must then also be assessed in the light of the time of decision-making and the scope of the information available at this time. It is also necessary to measure the intensity of the reasons for the state of legislative emergency in relation to the limitations of the constitutional principles concerned. It must be clearly and without any doubt clear what specific consequences, according to the government, are at risk for the values defined in this provision. Thus, in our particular case, it is a statement that justifies the conclusion on the threat of significant economic damage, whether we would compensate our pensioners for the rising prices, as the law so far says. I feel that no economic damage would arise, on the contrary, that a lot of pensioners, a lot of seniors would just have money for either food, medicine or rent. That's all that matters. None of this has been told by the government, and declaring a state of legislative emergency is simply a mockery of the constitution, the law and, in our view, an absolute outrage. In its findings, the Constitutional Court clearly states that it cannot be considered as sufficient reason to limit constitutional principles relating to the legislative process without further reference to a general and hypothetical risk based on no specific data, such as a reference such as that any failure to adopt a draft law could have a negative impact on the budget deficit, on the evaluation of credit rating agencies or international financial markets. And I add that the government is no helpless witness to budgetary events that are not natural laws, but that it is an institution that has all the tools in its hands to effectively combat the growing budget deficit on both the part of public income and public expenditure, without having to rob our seniors, disabled people, widows, widows or orphans. Each legislative process takes time. However, Parliament must be prepared for a situation where the legislative process needs to be accelerated. None of us are questioning that. But at the same time, we are strongly opposed to the abuse of this instrument, as the five-calice government is doing. And he doesn't do it for the first time. [[21.40 hours]] The Institute of Legislative Emergency was introduced into the House's Rules of Procedure at its very first adoption in 1995 because it was clear to all that a situation could arise, which is clearly given, clearly determined by three basic things, when in an accelerated process it would be necessary to adopt an amendment to the law in order to avoid significant damage. The Constitution contains no details concerning the length and procedure of the legislative process in the Chamber of Deputies. The Constitution does not regulate the number of readings or the time limit for the examination of the draft law in committees. The only detail of the parliamentary legislative process that the Constitution regulates is the 30-day suspension period within which the Senate has to discuss the law. For this reason, the Constitution did not contain any institutes for shortening the legislative process, as this would prevent future legal regulation. At the time of the adoption of the Constitution in 1992, there was no law on the security of the Czech Republic, which had taken over the Institute of accelerated negotiations for inspiration from the House's Rules of Procedure into the constitutional text, with the fact that, in the case referred to in the Constitutional Security Act, when an emergency was declared, legislative emergency had not been declared, and yet a shortened negotiation of the bill could have been held. Short-term legislative negotiations have been introduced in the context of emergencies - a state of war and a state of danger - directly by the constitution, because it contains a shortening of that constitutional, Senate 30-day deadline. This regulation of short-term conduct at constitutional level had no bearing on what the legislative process should look like in other cases. It did not modify provisions which would imply that, in other, normal cases, the legislative process could not be shortened or could be reduced. By the amendment of the Constitution in 1999, emergency situations were designed to regulate the accelerated negotiations so that it was not necessary in these cases to declare a legislative emergency in advance. In fact, the general procedural regulation of the accelerated negotiations has been simplified to cases of external security. In doing so, account has been taken of the past practice, which was based on the fact that, if a very rigidly revised state of legislative emergency is adopted, the accelerated negotiations in emergency situations could also be applied for the purpose of the State's security. The Constitutional Act on Security of the Czech Republic thus took advantage of the knowledge with the application of the state of legislative emergency and shortened negotiations and anchored it in a modified form into a constitutional amendment. The state of the legislative emergency following the adoption of the Act on the Rules of Procedure of the Chamber of Deputies, in 1995, was therefore the only instrument for the accelerated process, except in the case of the adoption of the Act on the basis of a decision of the Security Council, when there was no need to declare the state of the legislative emergency at all. It should also be noted that the Rules of Procedure introduced three new laws in 1995 and thus the legislative process was considerably extended. Today, we know that the legislative process for the adoption of the law, including the approval of the Senate and the signature of the President, may take eleven months. At that time, however, there was no Senate. The length of the legislative process did not bring such great difficulty, because the laws of the Chamber of Deputies acted directly to the President of the Republic. Yet, in 1995, the state of legislative emergency was used. In addition to the state of legislative emergency, it was possible to shorten the legislative process in another way, which has limited possibilities after the Senate was established. As today, it was possible to shorten the 60-day deadline for the discussion of proposals in committees with the agreement of the appellant to one day. It was also possible to discuss the bill in three readings at a single meeting of the House. Until the Senate existed, it was possible to discuss, approve the Government's bill within a few days. In 2000, the Institute was accepted to agree with the draft law at first reading by amending Section 90 of the Rules of Procedure of the House, which states in paragraph 2: At the same time as the draft law, the proposer may propose to the House that the draft law be approved at first reading. The justification for such a bill must be given in the explanatory memorandum. If the reason is the implementation of the obligations arising from the contracts by which the Czech Republic is bound, the petitioner will submit a full translation of the legal standards to be implemented. However, this institute, if proposed, can be vetoed by two parliamentary clubs in contrast to the state of legislative emergency. This is a fundamental difference and also an essential instrument for protecting the parliamentary minority and citizens, which it represents against the will of the parliamentary majority. Such a defence instrument does not contain a state of legislative emergency, and therefore it is necessary to treat it with high caution and not abuse it. Some parliaments in Europe, such as Germany, have limited the state of legislative emergency to just a few attempts during the parliamentary term, such as two or three. Under the Rules of Procedure of the House, the Government shall invite the President of the House to declare a state of legislative emergency for a certain period of time. The Chamber of Deputies must confirm, abolish or limit the state of legislative emergency. However, the President of the House may reject the Government's proposal to discuss the government's proposals in an accelerated negotiation and may not allow legislative emergency law to be negotiated. So it's also partly up to the President if she admits to negotiating legislation in a legislative emergency - she has already admitted - so the House will vote. [[He's turning to the chair.]] And I say, Madame Pekarová, Adamová, that is also a challenge for you. Don't you want to make a positive entry into the history of Czech parliamentarianism? The House may also refuse to discuss the law in a legislative emergency by not confirming in the vote at the beginning of the meeting that the situation persists. On this point, the President of the House shall open a debate in which Members may comment, may put forward various procedural proposals, voting on all these proposals by the House. Before discussing each draft law, the House shall decide in the vote whether there are conditions for short-term negotiations. If she finds that the conditions have passed, the bill cannot be dealt with in this way. On this point too, a debate is open. In a state of legislative emergency, only a government proposal based on government legislative rules can be discussed, and a detailed analysis of the legal and factual situation must be preceded by this proposal. Has all this been complied with in the case of the amendment to the Pension Insurance Act? Well, it doesn't look like it yet. The enormous risk of a state of legislative emergency, as we see it literally live, is the ease of abuse of this institute, because it does not allow the parliamentary opposition, i.e. Members who are currently in a minority, to block negotiations on the bill, as is the case with the proper negotiation of the bill at first reading. The state of legislative emergency was originally designed for a completely different purpose than the force of government's repression of controversial and debatable proposals, namely, for the adoption of non-conflict bills in exceptional circumstances, when it was necessary to act quickly - yes, right, in exceptional circumstances - proposals on which political and social consensus prevails. Is this the case of a motion to reduce pension valorisation? No way. Every judicial person must acknowledge that the draconian restrictive conditions of the state of legislative emergency for discussing the bill, which is designed to significantly limit the social rights of our seniors and their standard of living, is far beyond the principles of democracy and the rule of law. [[21.50 hours]] The constitutional conformity of the legislative process in view of declaring a state of legislative emergency has already been dealt with several times by our Constitutional Court. The drafters of individual constitutional complaints most often saw the abuse of the institute of legislative emergency in that the conditions for its publication were not met under the relevant provisions of the Rules of Procedure of the Chamber of Deputies, which they considered not only as a violation of this law but also as a violation of the parliamentary procedure establishing an unconstitutional way of adopting the law. The announcement of a state of legislative emergency in the relevant complaints was then identified as a political, not neutral legal instrument and a limitation of the general or detailed debate as a significant restriction on the freedom of expression of Members and the freedom of parliamentary debate. In the decision by the Constitutional Court in 2011, the amendment of Act No. 347/2010 Coll., which amends certain laws in the context of austerity measures under the jurisdiction of the Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs, has also been pointed out by foreign case-law dealing with a number of constitutional democratic principles of the legislative process, which must be followed by the legislature. In the finding, the Constitutional Court once again stated the principles that the legislature must respect when examining draft laws. They include, above all, respect for procedural rules or, if you want procedural rules. The Constitutional Court states - I quote: "Only in a process flawless process, a legal and constitutionally consistent outcome can be obtained in constitutionally consistent proceedings, i.e. a decision, and therefore the procedural purity of the decision-making process of the proceedings needs to be given increased attention and to provide it with strong protection." That is why I dare to come up with this and actually discuss it in such a thorough way here, because it is clear that if there were an abuse of the institute of legislative emergency, we would probably have met at the Constitutional Court, probably, and for that reason, I am also calling attention to the judiciary of the Constitutional Court, which says what will be considered in the event of a violation of the legislative emergency. "Therefore, in the legislative process, the requirement of permanence, persuasion and the necessity of legal acts, on which the rule of law and the combined life of citizens rests. However, such acts and also the achievement of the necessary authority of the legislatures cannot be achieved by any other means than by respect for the rules, i.e. the principles of legislative activity, which the Chamber of Deputies, as an important legislator, established by law for this activity." End of quote. Let us bear this in mind today and avoid the risk of a later abolition of today's amendment by the Constitutional Court, in addition to the reasons of content for procedural reasons, i.e. for the reasons we are discussing today, that is to say, the use of the Institute of Legislative Emergency. Parliamentary opposition, which does not participate directly in the exercise of state power and is in a minority in Parliament, must be allowed to exercise its constitutionally guaranteed rights in view of the constitution guaranteed by the principle of protection of minorities. It must not be prevented from exercising the function of parliamentary opposition. As the Constitutional Court finds, the present - and I quote - the presence of the opposition also forces a coherent government majority to explain and justify their decisions based on a free exchange of views and opinions in order to seek a wider consensus and, where appropriate, to remedy the shortcomings of the proposed law. That's what this is all about. That's what the journalists asked us: "Why are you going to talk for so long? Because we want to convince the government coalition that this law can be adopted otherwise, that we can achieve some kind of compromise and that we do not have to reduce or slow down the valorisation of our seniors' pensions, because they will lose quite a lot of money, one third, and that, of course, we don't think it's okay. The opposition also has the right to block or delay decisions taken by the majority, as well as the right to seek constitutional review of decisions and laws adopted. That's probably what we're gonna do. If the government's majority pushes this law, we will, of course, give it to the Constitutional Court to investigate whether, for example, there has been an abuse of the state of legislative emergency. The level of protection of the rights of the opposition, regardless of whether it is politically constituted at the moment in order to fulfil its irreplaceable function, is an indicator of the level of political and parliamentary culture and is also an expression of the degree of democraticity of the political system in question. Yes, the fact that we can talk here today and that we can talk here for so long is due to the Law on Rules of Procedure, and it is, as the Constitutional Court directly says, an expression of the degree of democraticity of the political system in question. That's important. There is always a limitation or reduction in the rights of the parliamentary opposition in the application of the Institute of Legislative Emergency. That's important, too. This is all the more serious when it comes to a proposal with so far-reaching implications for the situation of millions of citizens, such as the amendment to the approval of pensions. The constitutionalist, therefore, decided, and the guardian of constitutionality the Constitutional Court confirms this, this possibility to admit only in extreme, really extraordinary situations. The Constitutional Court therefore urges a very restrictive interpretation of the conditions and circumstances leading to the use of the Institute of Legislative Emergency in dealing with bills. The draft law, which the Government requires in the shortened legislative process, must therefore be an appropriate means of averting any harm or endangering the public interest. I am convinced that our seniors are neither a threat to the public interest nor a threat to the public interest, which is absolutely not true in the case of the amendment to the Pension Insurance Act. Absolutely not! On the contrary.
